<div class='question question'>

I have some $20\ \text{p}$ pieces. I have twice as 
many $10\ \text{p}$ pieces as $20\ \text{p}$ pieces. I have 
twice as many $5\ \text{p}$ pieces as I 
have $10\ \text{p}$ pieces. I have $\pounds 4.80$ altogether. 
How many coins do I have?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number of $20\ \text{p}$ coins $= a$

Number $10 \ \text{p}$ coins $= 2a$

Number $5\ \text{p}$ coins $= 4a$

ABBR: It is really important to use the right units in calculations and convert where required
. Let's convert the total amount into pence.

$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Total amount} &= \pounds 4.80\\\\
                    &= 4.80 \times 100 \ \text{p} \\\\
                    &= 480 \ \text{p}
\end{aligned}
$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
20 \times a + 10 \times 2a + 5 \times 4a    &= 480\\\\
20a + 20a + 20a                             &= 480 \\\\
60a                                         &= 480 \\\\
a                                           &= \dfrac {480} {60} \\\\
a                                           &= \dfrac {2 \times 2 \times 3 \times 5 \times 8} {2 \times 2 \times 3 \times 5} \\\\
a                                           &= \dfrac {\cancel 2 \times \cancel 2 \times \cancel 3 \times \cancel 5 \times 8} {\cancel 2 \times \cancel 2 \times \cancel 3 \times \cancel 5} \\\\
a                                           &= 8
\end{aligned}
$

$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Total coins}  &= a + 2a + 4a \\\\
                    &= 7a \\\\
                    &= 7 \times 8 \\\\
                    &= 56 \ \text{coins}
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$56$ coins

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number of $20\ \text{p}$ coins $= a$
<emptyline>
Number $10 \ \text{p}$ coins $= 2a$
<emptyline>
Number $5\ \text{p}$ coins $= 4a$
<emptyline>

<emptyline>

\begin{aligned}

\end{aligned}
$

a                                           &= \dfrac {2 \times 2 \times 3 \times 5 \times 8} {2 \times 2 \times 3 \times 5} \\\\
a                                           &= \dfrac {\cancel 2 \times \cancel 2 \times \cancel 3 \times \cancel 5 \times 8} {\cancel 2 \times \cancel 2 \times \cancel 3 \times \cancel 5} \\\\
a                                           &= 8
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}

$

</div>
</div>

<div class='question question'>

Irfan is standing in a queue of $24$ people.

What position is he in the queue if there are $7$ more people ahead of him than behind him?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number of people behind Irfan $= a$

The number of people ahead of Irfan $= a + 7$

So, *People ahead* $+$ *Irfan* $+$ *People behind* $= 24$

Putting this into an equation, we get

$
\begin{aligned}
(a + 7) + 1 + a     &= 24 \\\\
a + 7 + 1 + a       &= 24 \\\\
2a + 8              &= 24 \\\\
2a                  &= 24 - 8 \\\\
2a                  &= 16 \\\\
a                   &= \dfrac {16} {2} \\\\
a                   &= 8
\end{aligned}
$

Number of people behind Irfan $= 8$. So,

Irfan's position $= 24 - 8 = 16$

Irfan is $16 \text{th}$ in the queue.

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$16$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number of people behind Irfan $= a$
<emptyline>
The number of people ahead of Irfan $= a + 7$
<emptyline>
So, *People ahead* $+$ *Irfan* $+$ *People behind* $= 24$
<emptyline>
Putting this into an equation, we get
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}

a + 7 + 1 + a       &= 24 \\\\
2a + 8              &= 24 \\\\
2a                  &= 24 - 8 \\\\
2a                  &= 16 \\\\
a                   &= \dfrac {16} {2} \\\\
a                   &= 8
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Number of people behind Irfan $= 8$. So,
<emptyline>
Irfan's position $= 24 - 8 = 16$
<emptyline>
Irfan is $16 \text{th}$ in the queue.
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$16$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<div class='uuid'>
<p>BFE40D96-8522-4C51-9255-AA907949965E</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2011--arithmetic-1:1:17</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>

<div class='rag'>

<div class='question question'>

Three CDs and two DVDs cost $\pounds 43$. Two CDs and three DVDs cost $\pounds 47$. What is the cost of one CD?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the cost of a CD in pounds $= c$

Let the cost of a DVD in pounds $= d$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned} 
3c + 2d &= 43 \\\\
2c + 3d &= 47
\end{aligned}
$

We have got $2$ variables and we have $2$ equations to solve them. Let's eliminate to reduce it to just one variable. 

But which one should we eliminate? If we look at the question again, we only need the 
cost for a CD, so let's eliminate $d$ so we can directly calculate the value for $c$.

The LCM of $2$ and $3$ is $6$. Multiply the first equation by $3$ and the second equation by $2$ to make the coeifficent of $d$ the same.

$
\begin{aligned} 
3 \times 3c + 3 \times 2d   &= 3 \times 43  \\\\
2 \times 2c + 2 \times 3d   &= 2 \times 47  \\\\
9c + 6d                     &= 129 \\\\
4c + 6d                     &= 94
\end{aligned}
$

Subtract the second equation from the first to reduce it to one variable.

$
\begin{aligned} 
9c + 6d - (4c + 6d) &= 129 - 94 \\\\
9c + 6d - 4c - 6d   &= 35 \\\\
5c                  &= 35 \\\\
c                   &= \dfrac {35} {5} \\\\
c                   &= \dfrac {7 \times 5} {5} \\\\
c                   &= \dfrac {7 \times \cancel{5}} {\cancel{5}} \\\\
c                   &= 7
\end{aligned}
$

Cost of a CD $= \pounds 7$

</div>
<div class='working'>

What if we eliminate $c$ instead?

This approach is less efficient as we will have to calculate one extra value to get the answer.

Let's eliminate to reduce it to just one variable. 

The LCM of $2$ and $3$ is $6$. Multiply the first equation by $2$ and the second equation by $3$ to make the coeifficent of $c$ the same.

$
\begin{aligned} 
2 \times 3c + 2 \times 2d   &= 2 \times 43 \\\\
3 \times 2c + 3 \times 3d   &= 3 \times 47 \\\\
6c + 4d                     &= 86 \\\\
6c + 9d                     &= 141
\end{aligned}
$

Subtract the first equation from the second to reduce it to one variable.

$
\begin{aligned} 
6c + 9d - (6c + 4d)     &= 141 - 86 \\\\
6c + 9d - 6c - 4d       &= 55 \\\\
5d                      &= 55 \\\\
d                       &= \dfrac {55} {5} \\\\
d                       &= \dfrac {11 \times 5} {5} \\\\
d                       &= \dfrac {11 \times \cancel{5}} {\cancel{5}} \\\\
d                       &= 11
\end{aligned}
$

Cost of a DVD $= \pounds 11$ 

Let's substitute the value of $d$ in one of the initial equations to find the cost of a CD.

$
\begin{aligned} 
3c + 2d             &= 43 \\\\
3c + 2 \times 11    &= 43 \\\\
3c + 22             &= 43 \\\\
3c                  &= 43 -22 \\\\
3c                  &= 21 \\\\
c                   &= \dfrac {21} {3} \\\\
c                   &= \dfrac {7 \times 3} {3} \\\\
c                   &= \dfrac {7 \times \cancel{3}} {\cancel{3}} \\\\
c                   &= 7
\end{aligned}
$

Cost of a CD $= \pounds 7$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$\pounds 7$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the cost of a CD in pounds $= c$
<emptyline>
Let the cost of a DVD in pounds $= d$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>

\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>

<emptyline>
But which one should we eliminate? If we look at the question again, we only need the 
cost for a CD, so let's eliminate $d$ so we can directly calculate the value for $c$.
<emptyline>
The LCM of $2$ and $3$ is $6$. Multiply the first equation by $3$ and the second equation by $2$ to make the coeifficent of $d$ the same.
<emptyline>

<emptyline>

\begin{aligned}

c                   &= \dfrac {7 \times 5} {5} \\\\
c                   &= \dfrac {7 \times \cancel{5}} {\cancel{5}} \\\\
c                   &= 7
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Cost of a CD $= \pounds 7$
</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>What if we eliminate $c$ instead?
<emptyline>

<emptyline>

<emptyline>

<div class='question_envelope rag_g1 rag_prns question'>
<div class='uuid'>
<p>7E7878A4-808C-4404-8B60-2FB05E498D2D</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2012--arithmetic-1:1:6</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_g1</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_prns</p>
</div>
<div class='topics'>
<ul>
<li>
talgebra
</li>
<li>
tmoney
</li>
<li>
tcoin
</li>
</ul>
</div>
<div class='question question'>

Ger has $\pounds 2$ made up of $20\ \text{p}$ and $10\ \text{p}$ coins. He has twice as many $20\ \text{p}$ coins as $10\ \text{p}$ ones. How many $10\ \text{p}$ coins does he have?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the count of $10 \ \text{p}$ coins $= c$

As per the question,

Count of $20 \ \text{p}$ coins $= 2c$

ABBR: It is really important to use the right units in calculations and convert where required.


Let's convert the total amount into pence.

$
\begin{aligned} 
\text{Total Amount}     &=  \pounds 2 \\\\
                        &=  2 \times 100 \ \text{p} \\\\
                        &=  200 \ \text{p}
\end{aligned}
$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned} 
10 \times c + 20 \times 2c      &= 200 \\\\
10c + 40c                       &= 200 \\\\
50c                             &= 200 \\\\
c                               &= \dfrac{200} {50} \\\\
c                               &= \dfrac{50 \times 4} {50} \\\\
c                               &= \dfrac{\cancel{50} \times 4} {\cancel{50}} \\\\
c                               &= 4
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$4$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the count of $10 \ \text{p}$ coins $= c$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
Count of $20 \ \text{p}$ coins $= 2c$
<emptyline>
rtq_abbr_md_unit_note
<emptyline>
Let's convert the total amount into pence.
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned} 
\text{Total Amount}     &=  \pounds 2 \\\\
                        &=  2 \times 100 \ \text{p} \\\\
                        &=  200 \ \text{p}
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned} 
10 \times c + 20 \times 2c      &= 200 \\\\
10c + 40c                       &= 200 \\\\
50c                             &= 200 \\\\
c                               &= \dfrac{200} {50} \\\\
c                               &= \dfrac{50 \times 4} {50} \\\\
c                               &= \dfrac{\cancel{50} \times 4} {\cancel{50}} \\\\
c                               &= 4
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$4$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_g1 rag_prns question'>
<div class='uuid'>
<p>6E6B11E1-CFDF-43B5-9CF2-E7E17FFE2599</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2012--arithmetic-1:1:8</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_g1</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_prns</p>
</div>
<div class='topics'>
<ul>
<li>
talgebra
</li>
</ul>
</div>
<div class='question question'>

Alison has $8$ more pencils than rulers. If she has a total of $52$ pencils and rulers, how many rulers does she have?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the count of rulers $= r$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned} 
r + (r + 8)                                &= 52 \\\\
2r + 8                                     &= 52 \\\\
2r                                         &= 52 - 8 \\\\
2r                                         &= 44 \\\\
r                                          &= \dfrac {44}{2} \\\\
r                                          &= \dfrac {2 \times 22}{2} \\\\
r                                          &= \dfrac {\cancel {2} \times 22}{\cancel {2}} \\\\
r                                          &= 22
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$22$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the count of rulers $= r$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned} 
r + (r + 8)                                &= 52 \\\\
2r + 8                                     &= 52 \\\\
2r                                         &= 52 - 8 \\\\
2r                                         &= 44 \\\\
r                                          &= \dfrac {44}{2} \\\\
r                                          &= \dfrac {2 \times 22}{2} \\\\
r                                          &= \dfrac {\cancel {2} \times 22}{\cancel {2}} \\\\
r                                          &= 22
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$22$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_g1 rag_prns question'>
<div class='uuid'>
<p>521214EB-CA81-4E61-8F79-5E1BF8007C95</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2012--arithmetic-1:1:20</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_g1</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_prns</p>
</div>
<div class='topics'>
<ul>
<li>
talgebra
</li>
</ul>
</div>
<div class='question question'>

Jo writes down a two digit number. When she reverses the digits and subtracts the smaller 
number from the larger number the answer she gets is $36$. What is the difference between the 
two digits in her original number?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the tens digit for the number $= a$

Let the unit digit for the number $= b$

Value of the original number $= 10a + b$

When the digits are reversed, the value of the reversed number $= 10b + a$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
(10a + b)- (10b + a)    &= 36 \\\\
10a + b- 10b - a        &= 36 \\\\
9a -9b                  &= 36 \\\\
9 (a - b)               &= 36 \\\\
a - b                   &= \dfrac {36}{9} \\\\
a - b                   &= 4
\end{aligned}
$

The difference between the two digits in her original number is $= 4$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$4$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the tens digit for the number $= a$
<emptyline>
Let the unit digit for the number $= b$
<emptyline>
Value of the original number $= 10a + b$
<emptyline>
When the digits are reversed, the value of the reversed number $= 10b + a$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
(10a + b)- (10b + a)    &= 36 \\\\
10a + b- 10b - a        &= 36 \\\\
9a -9b                  &= 36 \\\\
9 (a - b)               &= 36 \\\\
a - b                   &= \dfrac {36}{9} \\\\
a - b                   &= 4
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
The difference between the two digits in her original number is $= 4$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$4$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<div class='question question'>

Neil thinks of a number, he halves it and then subtracts $9$.  If the answer he gets is $12$, what was the original number he thought of?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number Neil thought of $= a$

As per the question,

$
\begin {aligned}
\dfrac {a} {2} - 9      &= 12 \\\\
\dfrac {a} {2}          &= 12 + 9 \\\\
\dfrac {a} {2}          &= 21 \\\\
a                       &= 21 \times 2 \\\\
a                       &= 42
\end {aligned}
$

The number Neil thought of $= 42$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$42$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number Neil thought of $= a$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin {aligned}
\dfrac {a} {2} - 9      &= 12 \\\\
\dfrac {a} {2}          &= 12 + 9 \\\\
\dfrac {a} {2}          &= 21 \\\\
a                       &= 21 \times 2 \\\\
a                       &= 42
\end {aligned}
$
<emptyline>
The number Neil thought of $= 42$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>

<div class='question_envelope rag_g1 rag_prns question'>
<div class='uuid'>
<p>1A6AB009-2F49-47AF-BA34-6312AA658C67</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2013--arithmetic-1:1:8</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_g1</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>

<div class='question question'>

John the electrician needs to cut three lengths of wire from the $5 \ \text{metre}$ roll he has brought with him to do a job. The three lengths he cuts are $85 \ \text{cm}$, $2.5 \ \text{metres}$ and $650 \ \text{mm}$. How much wire (in cms) will he have left after cutting off his lengths?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the left over wire (in $\text {cm}$) $= w$.

ABBR: It is really important to use the right units in calculations and convert where required
. The wire measurement are given in multiple units.

Let's convert all the measurements into $\text{cm}$.

As per the question,

$
\begin {aligned}
85 \ \text {cm} +  2.5 \ \text {metre} + 650 \ \text {mm} + w                                               &= 5 \ \text {metre} \\\\
85 \ \text {cm} +  2.5 \times 100 \ \text {cm} + \dfrac {650} {10} \ \text {cm} + w                         &= 5 \times 100 \ \text {cm} \\\\
85 \ \text {cm} +  250 \ \text {cm} + \dfrac {65 \cancel {0}} {1 \cancel {0}} \ \text {cm} + w              &= 500 \ \text {cm} \\\\
85 \ \text {cm} +  250 \ \text {cm} + 65 \ \text {cm} + w                                                   &= 500 \ \text {cm} \\\\
85  + 250  + 65 + w                                                                                         &= 500  \\\\
400 + w                                                                                                     &= 500 \\\\
w                                                                                                           &= 500 - 400 \\\\
w                                                                                                           &= 100 \ \text {cm}
\end {aligned}
$

The left over wire length is = $100\ \text {cm}$.

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$100 \ \text {cm}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the left over wire (in $\text {cm}$) $= w$.
<emptyline>
rtq_abbr_md_unit_no_dot_note. The wire measurement are given in multiple units.
<emptyline>
Let's convert all the measurements into $\text{cm}$.
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin {aligned}
85 \ \text {cm} +  2.5 \ \text {metre} + 650 \ \text {mm} + w                                               &= 5 \ \text {metre} \\\\
85 \ \text {cm} +  2.5 \times 100 \ \text {cm} + \dfrac {650} {10} \ \text {cm} + w                         &= 5 \times 100 \ \text {cm} \\\\
85 \ \text {cm} +  250 \ \text {cm} + \dfrac {65 \cancel {0}} {1 \cancel {0}} \ \text {cm} + w              &= 500 \ \text {cm} \\\\
85 \ \text {cm} +  250 \ \text {cm} + 65 \ \text {cm} + w                                                   &= 500 \ \text {cm} \\\\
85  + 250  + 65 + w                                                                                         &= 500  \\\\
400 + w                                                                                                     &= 500 \\\\
w                                                                                                           &= 500 - 400 \\\\
w                                                                                                           &= 100 \ \text {cm}
\end {aligned}
$
<emptyline>
The left over wire length is = $100\ \text {cm}$.
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$100 \ \text {cm}$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<div class='question_envelope rag_g1 rag_prns question'>
<div class='uuid'>
<p>71FE7449-4F1E-4FA7-AA3E-E13ECDAD6EF7</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2013--arithmetic-1:1:10</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_g1</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_prns</p>
</div>
<div class='topics'>
<ul>
<li>
talgebra
</li>
<li>
tmoney
</li>
</ul>
</div>
<div class='question question'>

Two shirts and three tops cost $\pounds 29$. Three shirts and one top cost $\pounds 19$. What is the cost of a top?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the price of shirts $= a$

Let the price of the top $= b$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
2a + 3b &= \pounds 29\\\\           
3a + 1b &= \pounds 19
\end{aligned}
$

We have got 2 variables and we have 2 equations to solve them .Let's use elimination to reduce it to just one variable. 

Multiply second equation by 3.

$
\begin{aligned}
3 \times 3a + 3 \times 1b  &= 3 \times 19 \\\\
9a + 3b                    &= 57
\end{aligned}
$

Subtract the first equation from the second to reduce it to one variable.

$
\begin{aligned}
9a + 3b - (2a + 3b) &= 57 - 29 \\\\
9a + 3b - 2a - 3b   &= 28 \\\\
7a                  &= 28 \\\\
a                   &= \dfrac {28} {7} \\\\
a                   &= \dfrac {4 \times 7} {7} \\\\
a                   &= \dfrac {4 \times \cancel 7} {\cancel 7} \\\\
a                   &= \pounds 4
\end{aligned}
$

Lets substitute the value of a in one of the equations.

$
\begin{aligned}           
3 \times 4 + 1b &= 19 \\\\
12 + b          &= 19 \\\\
b               &= 19 - 12 \\\\
b               &= \pounds 7
\end{aligned}
$

The cost of a shirt $= \pounds 4$

The cost of a top $=\pounds 7$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$\pounds 7$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the price of shirts $= a$
<emptyline>
Let the price of the top $= b$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
2a + 3b &= \pounds 29\\\\           
3a + 1b &= \pounds 19
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
We have got 2 variables and we have 2 equations to solve them .Let's use elimination to reduce it to just one variable. 
<emptyline>
Multiply second equation by 3.
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
3 \times 3a + 3 \times 1b  &= 3 \times 19 \\\\
9a + 3b                    &= 57
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Subtract the first equation from the second to reduce it to one variable.
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
9a + 3b - (2a + 3b) &= 57 - 29 \\\\
9a + 3b - 2a - 3b   &= 28 \\\\
7a                  &= 28 \\\\
a                   &= \dfrac {28} {7} \\\\
a                   &= \dfrac {4 \times 7} {7} \\\\
a                   &= \dfrac {4 \times \cancel 7} {\cancel 7} \\\\
a                   &= \pounds 4
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Lets substitute the value of a in one of the equations.
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}           
3 \times 4 + 1b &= 19 \\\\
12 + b          &= 19 \\\\
b               &= 19 - 12 \\\\
b               &= \pounds 7
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
The cost of a shirt $= \pounds 4$
<emptyline>
The cost of a top $=\pounds 7$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$\pounds 7$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

</div>
<div class='question question'>

Two metal weights in the form of cuboids have the same volume.  One is $20 \ \text{cm}$ long, $16 \ \text{cm}$ wide and $9 \ \text{cm}$ high. 
The other weight is $10 \ \text{cm}$ wide and $12 \ \text{cm}$ high. 

What is its length if the volume of a cuboid is given by $\text{length} \times \text{width} \times \text{height}?$

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the length of the second cuboid (in $\text{cm}$) $= l$

ABBR: $\text{Volume of a cuboid} = \text{length} \times \text{width} \times \text{height}$


As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
10 \times 12 \times l   &=20 \times 16 \times 9 \\\\
l                       &= \dfrac {20 \times 16 \times 9} {10 \times 12} \\\\
l                       &= \dfrac {2 \times 10 \times 4 \times 4 \times 3 \times 3} {10 \times 4 \times 3} \\\\
l                       &= \dfrac {2 \times 1\cancel{0} \times \cancel{4} \times 4 \times \cancel{3} \times 3} {1\cancel{0} \times \cancel{4} \times \cancel{3}} \\\\
l                       &= 2 \times 4 \times 3 \\\\
l                       &= 24 \ \text{cm}
\end{aligned}
$

Length of the second cuboid $= 24 \ \text{cm}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$24 \ \text{cm}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the length of the second cuboid (in $\text{cm}$) $= l$
<emptyline>
rtq_abbr_katex_formula_volume_cuboid
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
10 \times 12 \times l   &=20 \times 16 \times 9 \\\\
l                       &= \dfrac {20 \times 16 \times 9} {10 \times 12} \\\\
l                       &= \dfrac {2 \times 10 \times 4 \times 4 \times 3 \times 3} {10 \times 4 \times 3} \\\\
l                       &= \dfrac {2 \times 1\cancel{0} \times \cancel{4} \times 4 \times \cancel{3} \times 3} {1\cancel{0} \times \cancel{4} \times \cancel{3}} \\\\
l                       &= 2 \times 4 \times 3 \\\\
l                       &= 24 \ \text{cm}
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Length of the second cuboid $= 24 \ \text{cm}$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>

<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2013--arithmetic-1:1:16</p>

<div class='topics'>

tcoin

<div class='question question'>

When I emptied my pockets recently I found I had a mixture of $10\ \text{p}$, $20\ \text{p}$, $50\ \text{p}$ and $\pounds 1$ coins. 
When I counted the value of all the coins, the total amount came to $\pounds 7.20$ and there were equal numbers of each coin. 

How many coins were there altogether?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the count of coins of each denomination $= c$

ABBR: It is really important to use the right units in calculations and convert where required
. Let's convert the total amount into pence.

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
10 \ \text{p} \times c + 20\ \text{p} \times c + 50\ \text{p} \times c + 100\ \text{p} \times c   &= \pounds 7.20 \\\\
10 \ \text{p} \times c + 20\ \text{p} \times c + 50\ \text{p} \times c + 100\ \text{p} \times c   &= 7.20 \times 100\ \text{p} \\\\
10c + 20c + 50c + 100c                                                                    &= 720 \\\\
180c                                                                                      &= 720 \\\\
c                                                                                         &= \dfrac {720} { 180} \\\\
c                                                                                         &= \dfrac {180 \times 4} { 180} \\\\
c                                                                                         &= \dfrac {\cancel{180} \times 4} { \cancel{180}} \\\\
c                                                                                         &= 4
\end{aligned}
$ 

There are $4$ coins of each type and $4$ different types of coins.

$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Total coins}   &=  4 \times 4 \\\\
                     &= 16 \ \text{coins}
\end{aligned}
$   

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$16$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the count of coins of each denomination $= c$
<emptyline>
rtq_abbr_md_unit_no_dot_note. Let's convert the total amount into pence.
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}

10 \ \text{p} \times c + 20\ \text{p} \times c + 50\ \text{p} \times c + 100\ \text{p} \times c   &= 7.20 \times 100\ \text{p} \\\\
10c + 20c + 50c + 100c                                                                    &= 720 \\\\
180c                                                                                      &= 720 \\\\
c                                                                                         &= \dfrac {720} { 180} \\\\
c                                                                                         &= \dfrac {180 \times 4} { 180} \\\\
c                                                                                         &= \dfrac {\cancel{180} \times 4} { \cancel{180}} \\\\
c                                                                                         &= 4
\end{aligned}
$ 
<emptyline>
There are $4$ coins of each type and $4$ different types of coins.
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Total coins}   &=  4 \times 4 \\\\
                     &= 16 \ \text{coins}
\end{aligned}
$   
</code></pre>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$16$
</code></pre>
</div>

<div class='question question'>

One of the ways to check if a number is divisible by $11$ is by alternately subtracting and adding its digits as follows.

Consider the digits in $121$. Since $1 - 2 + 1 = 0$ we can say that $121$ is divisible by $11$. 

For the number $16918$, the digits combine as $1 - 6 + 9 - 1 + 8 = 11$, which is a multiple of $11$ so $16918$ is divisible by $11$.

However for $147$, $1 - 4 + 7 = 4$, which is not in the $11$ times table, so $147$ is NOT divisible by $11$. 

Neither is $3276$ as $3 - 2 + 7 - 6 = 2$, which is also not a multiple of $11$.

By applying this process of subtracting and adding, answer the following questions

</div>




<ul class='subquestion lower-alpha question_type_subquestion list_type_lower-alpha'>
<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

Show that $62557$ is divisible by $11$.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

$6 - 2 + 5 - 5 + 7 = 11$

$11$ is a multiple of $11$ so $62557$ is divisible by $11$. 

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$6 - 2 + 5 - 5 + 7 = 11$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$6 - 2 + 5 - 5 + 7 = 11$
<emptyline>
$11$ is a multiple of $11$ so $62557$ is divisible by $11$. 
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$6 - 2 + 5 - 5 + 7 = 11$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

</div>
</li>
<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>

</div>
<div class='workings'>

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

In the number $74\text{n}130$ what value does the digit $\text{n}$ have to be, if the number is divisible by $11?$

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

$74n130$

$
\begin{aligned}
7 - 4 + n - 1 + 3 - 0 &= 11 \\\\
5 + n                 &= 11 \\\\
n                     &= 11 - 5 \\\\
n                     &= 6
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$n = 6$

</div>
</div>

\end{aligned}
$

<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

In the number $83\text{a}4b9$, the digit $\text{b}$ is three times digit $\text{a}$. Find values for $\text{a}$ and $\text{b}$ if the number is divisible by $11$.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

$83a4b9$

$b = 3a$

As per question,

$
\begin{aligned}
8 - 3 + a - 4 + b - 9  &= 0 \\\\
a + b - 8              &= 0 \\\\
a + 3a                 &= 8 \\\\
4a                     &= 8 \\\\
a                      &= \dfrac {8}{4} \\\\
a                      &= \dfrac {4 \times 2}{4} \\\\
a                      &= \dfrac {\cancel 4 \times 2}{\cancel 4} \\\\
a                      &= 2 
\end{aligned}
$

Lets use the value of a to find b.

$
\begin{aligned}
b   &= 3a \\\\
b   &= 3 \times 2 \\\\
b   &= 6
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$a = 2$

</div>
<div class='answer'>

$b = 6$

</div>
</div>

<div class='question question'>

The ancient "Viva" tribe of Central America combined numbers in a particular way. Their method used the following symbol $(.)$ and worked like this

$\text{a}\ (.)\ \text{b} = 5$ times $\text{a}$ plus $\text{b}$ (ie $5 \times \text{a} + \text{b}$)

so,

$2\ (.)\ 7 = 5 \times 2 + 7 = 17$ and $9\ (.)\ 3 = 5 \times 9 + 3 = 48$

Work out what the answer would be for these Viva calculations.

Work out what value the letter would have to have to give the following results in these Viva problems.

</div>

<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

If $\text{m}\ (.)\ 2 = 37$, what number must $\text{m}$ be?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

$m\ (.)\ 2 = 37$

$
\begin{aligned}
5 \times m + 2 &= 37 \\\\
5m             &= 37 - 2 \\\\
5m             &= 35 \\\\
m              &= \dfrac{35} {5} \\\\
m              &= \dfrac {5 \times 7} {5} \\\\
m              &= \dfrac {\cancel 5 \times 7} {\cancel 5} \\\\
m              &= 7 
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$m = 7$

</div>
</div>

$
</code></pre>

<div class='question subquestion'>

If $\text{n}\ (.)\ 4 = 8\ (.)\ \text{n}$, what number must $\text{n}$ be?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>



$
\begin{aligned}
n\ (.)\ 4        &= 8\ (.)\ n \\\\
5 \times n + 4   &= 5 \times 8 + n \\\\
(5 \times n) + 4 &= (5 \times 8) + n \\\\
5n + 4           &= 40 + n \\\\ 
5n - n           &= 40 - 4 \\\\         
4n               &= 36 \\\\
n                &= \dfrac{36} {9} \\\\
n                &= \dfrac{9 \times 4} {9} \\\\
n                &= \dfrac{\cancel 9 \times 4} {\cancel 9} \\\\
n                &= 9 
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$n = 9$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>

5n - n           &= 40 - 4 \\\\         
4n               &= 36 \\\\
n                &= \dfrac{36} {9} \\\\
n                &= \dfrac{9 \times 4} {9} \\\\
n                &= \dfrac{\cancel 9 \times 4} {\cancel 9} \\\\
n                &= 9 
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>

<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

$

S^2    &= 20 \times 45 \\\\

S      &= \sqrt{900} \\\\
S      &= \sqrt{30 \times 30} \\\\
S      &= 30
\end{aligned}
$

The speed of the ball is $30$ m/s.

When the speed is $40 \ \text{m/s}$, how far has the ball fallen?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
S^2          &= 20 \times d \\\\
40^2         &= 20 \times d \\\\
20 \times d  &= 40 \times 40 \\\\
d            &= \dfrac {40 \times 40}{20} \\\\
d            &= \dfrac {40 \times 20 \times 2}{20} \\\\
d            &= \dfrac {40 \times \cancel {20} \times 2}{\cancel {20}} \\\\
d            &= 40 \times 2 \\\\
d            &= 80 \ \text{m}
\end{aligned}
$



</div>

\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

For football at Rovers ground an adult ticket costs $\pounds 15$ and a child's ticket costs $\pounds 4$. Find the total cost for $20$ adults and $12$ children.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>


$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Total cost}  &= \text {Adult ticket cost} \times \text {Number of adults} + \text {Child ticket} \times \text {Number of children} \\\\
                    &= (20 \times 15) + (12 \times 4) \\\\
                    &= 300 + 48 \\\\
                    &= \pounds 348
\end{aligned}
$



</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$\pounds 348$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>
$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Total cost}  &= \text {Adult ticket cost} \times \text {Number of adults} + \text {Child ticket} \times \text {Number of children} \\\\
                    &= (20 \times 15) + (12 \times 4) \\\\
                    &= 300 + 48 \\\\
                    &= \pounds 348
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>

</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$\pounds 348$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

</div>
</li>
<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

For a rugby match, $30$ adults and $16$ children paid $\pounds 408$ to see the game. A child's ticket cost $\pounds 3$. Work out the cost of one adult ticket.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>


Let the cost of adult ticket $= a$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
30 \times a + 16 \times 3  &= 408 \\\\
30a + 48                   &= 408 \\\\
30a                        &= 408 - 48 \\\\
30a                        &= 360 \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {360} {30} \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {12 \times 30} {30} \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {12 \times \cancel {30}}{\cancel {30}} \\\\
a                          &= \pounds 12
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$\pounds 12$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>
Let the cost of adult ticket $= a$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
30 \times a + 16 \times 3  &= 408 \\\\
30a + 48                   &= 408 \\\\
30a                        &= 408 - 48 \\\\
30a                        &= 360 \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {360} {30} \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {12 \times 30} {30} \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {12 \times \cancel {30}}{\cancel {30}} \\\\
a                          &= \pounds 12
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$\pounds 12$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

</div>
</li>
<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

$50$ adults and $40$ children went to the velodrome for the cycling championships. The total bill for the tickets was $\pounds 760$. An adult ticket cost three times as much as a child's ticket. Work out the cost of an adult ticket.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>


Let the cost of a child ticket $= a$

Cost of adult ticket $= 3a$

As per question, 

$
\begin{aligned}
50 \times 3a + 40 \times a &= 760 \\\\
150a + 40a                 &= 760 \\\\
190a                       &= 760 \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac{760} {190} \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {19 \times 4 \times 10} {19 \times 10} \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {\cancel {19} \times 4 \times \cancel {10}} {\cancel {19} \times \cancel {10}} \\\\
a                          &= \pounds 4
\end{aligned}
$

$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Cost of adult ticket} &= 3 \times a \\\\
                             &= 3 \times 4 \\\\
                             &= \pounds 12
\end{aligned}
$



</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$\pounds 12$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>
Let the cost of a child ticket $= a$
<emptyline>
Cost of adult ticket $= 3a$
<emptyline>

<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
50 \times 3a + 40 \times a &= 760 \\\\

190a                       &= 760 \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac{760} {190} \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {19 \times 4 \times 10} {19 \times 10} \\\\
a                          &= \dfrac {\cancel {19} \times 4 \times \cancel {10}} {\cancel {19} \times \cancel {10}} \\\\
a                          &= \pounds 4
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Cost of adult ticket} &= 3 \times a \\\\
                             &= 3 \times 4 \\\\
                             &= \pounds 12
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>

</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$\pounds 12$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

</div>
</li>
<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

At the swimming tournament a child's ticket cost $\pounds 6$ less than an adult ticket. 
The total cost of the tickets for $25$ adults and $30$ children was $\pounds 370$. Work out the cost of one child ticket.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the cost of a adult ticket $= a$

Cost of a child ticket $= a - 6$

As per question,

$
\begin{aligned}
25 \times a + 30 \times (a - 6) &= 370 \\\\
25a + 30a - 180                 &= 370 \\\\
55a - 180                       &= 370 \\\\
55a                             &= 370 + 180 \\\\
55a                             &= 550 \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {550} {55} \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {55 \times 10} {55} \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {\cancel {55} \times 10} {\cancel {55}} \\\\
a                               &= \pounds 10
\end{aligned}
$

$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Cost of a child ticket} &= a - 6 \\\\
                               &= 10 - 6 \\\\
                               &= \pounds 4
\end{aligned}
$


</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$\pounds 4$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the cost of a adult ticket $= a$
<emptyline>
Cost of a child ticket $= a - 6$
<emptyline>

55a                             &= 370 + 180 \\\\
55a                             &= 550 \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {550} {55} \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {55 \times 10} {55} \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {\cancel {55} \times 10} {\cancel {55}} \\\\
a                               &= \pounds 10
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Cost of a child ticket} &= a - 6 \\\\
                               &= 10 - 6 \\\\
                               &= \pounds 4
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>

</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$\pounds 4$
</code></pre>
</div>

<div class='uuid'>
<p>BF5B5BD3-287F-4180-939C-D53BE202DA6C</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2014--arithmetic-a:1:15</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>

<div class='rag'>

<div class='topics'>
<ul>

</li>
</ul>
</div>
<div class='question question'>

A cow eats $8  \ \text{kg}$ of food per day and a pig eats $6  \ \text{kg}$ of 
food per day. A farmer has $30$ cows and some pigs.  If the animals 
eat a total of $360  \ \text{kg}$ of food each day, work out how 
many pigs the farmer has.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number of pigs $= a$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned} 
30 \times 8 + a \times 6    &= 360 \\\\
240 + 6a                    &= 360 \\\\
6a                          &= 360 - 240 \\\\
6a                          &= 120 \\\\
a                           &= \dfrac {120} {6} \\\\
a                           &= \dfrac {20 \times 6} {6} \\\\
a                           &= \dfrac {20 \times \cancel{6}} {\cancel{6}} \\\\
a                           &= 20
\end{aligned}
$

Number of pigs $= 20$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$20$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number of pigs $= a$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned} 
30 \times 8 + a \times 6    &= 360 \\\\
240 + 6a                    &= 360 \\\\
6a                          &= 360 - 240 \\\\
6a                          &= 120 \\\\
a                           &= \dfrac {120} {6} \\\\
a                           &= \dfrac {20 \times 6} {6} \\\\
a                           &= \dfrac {20 \times \cancel{6}} {\cancel{6}} \\\\
a                           &= 20
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Number of pigs $= 20$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$20$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<div class='question_envelope rag_g1 rag_prns question'>
<div class='uuid'>
<p>625257F8-46C4-4F5F-9DF6-C451EA995331</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2014--arithmetic-a:1:17</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_g1</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_prns</p>
</div>
<div class='topics'>
<ul>
<li>
talgebra
</li>
</ul>
</div>
<div class='question question'>

David cuts a $6  \ \text{metre}$ long pole into three different 
pieces.  The largest piece is $40 \ \text{cm}$ longer than the middle piece 
and the middle piece is $70 \ \text{cm}$ longer than the shortest piece.

What is the size of the largest piece?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the shortest piece (in $\text{cm}$) $= a$

Middle piece (in $\text{cm}$) $= a + 70$

Largest piece (in $\text{cm}$) $= a + 70 + 40$

ABBR: It is really important to use the right units in calculations and convert where required.

The pole measurement is given in metres while the measurement of the pieces are given in cms.
Let's convert the total length into $\text{cm}$.

$
\begin{aligned} 
\text{Total length}     &=  6  \ \text{metre} \\\\
                        &=  6 \times 100 \ \text{cm} \\\\
                        &=  600 \ \text{cm}
\end{aligned}
$

As per the question

$
\begin{aligned} 
a + (a + 70) + (a + 70 + 40)    &= 600 \\\\
a + a + 70 + a + 70 + 40        &= 600 \\\\
3a + 180                        &= 600 \\\\
3a                              &= 600 - 180\\\\
3a                              &= 420 \\\\
3a                              &= \dfrac {420} {3} \\\\
a                               &= 140 \ \text{cm}
\end{aligned}
$

The shortest piece $= 120$ cm

Let's calculate the largest piece.

$
\begin{aligned} 
\text{Largest piece}   &= a + 70 + 40 \\\\
                       &= 140 + 70 + 40 \\\\
                       &= 250  \ \text{cm}
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

Largest piece $=$ $250  \ \text{cm}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the shortest piece (in $\text{cm}$) $= a$
<emptyline>
Middle piece (in $\text{cm}$) $= a + 70$
<emptyline>
Largest piece (in $\text{cm}$) $= a + 70 + 40$
<emptyline>
rtq_abbr_md_unit_note
The pole measurement is given in metres while the measurement of the pieces are given in cms.
Let's convert the total length into $\text{cm}$.
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned} 
\text{Total length}     &=  6  \ \text{metre} \\\\
                        &=  6 \times 100 \ \text{cm} \\\\
                        &=  600 \ \text{cm}
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
As per the question
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned} 
a + (a + 70) + (a + 70 + 40)    &= 600 \\\\
a + a + 70 + a + 70 + 40        &= 600 \\\\
3a + 180                        &= 600 \\\\
3a                              &= 600 - 180\\\\
3a                              &= 420 \\\\
3a                              &= \dfrac {420} {3} \\\\
a                               &= 140 \ \text{cm}
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
The shortest piece $= 120$ cm
<emptyline>
Let's calculate the largest piece.
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned} 
\text{Largest piece}   &= a + 70 + 40 \\\\
                       &= 140 + 70 + 40 \\\\
                       &= 250  \ \text{cm}
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Largest piece</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$250  \ \text{cm}$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<div class='question_envelope rag_g1 rag_prns question'>
<div class='uuid'>
<p>E32C98B2-7C2C-406F-AEAC-A4AECCE46113</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2014--arithmetic-a:1:18</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_g1</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_prns</p>
</div>
<div class='topics'>
<ul>
<li>
talgebra
</li>
</ul>
</div>
<div class='question question'>

Two bags of crisps and five chocolate biscuits cost $\pounds 1.76$. If 
each bag of crisps costs  $4 \text{p}$ more than a chocolate 
biscuit, what is the cost of a biscuit?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the cost of a chocolate biscuit (in pence) $= a$

Cost of a bag of crisps (in pence) $= a + 4$

ABBR: It is really important to use the right units in calculations and convert where required.


Lets convert all the costs into pence (p).

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned} 
2 \times (a + 4) + 5 \times a   &= \pounds 1.76  \\\\
2 \times (a + 4) + 5 \times a   &= 1.76 \times 100 \ \text{p}  \\\\
2 \times (a + 4) + 5 \times a   &= 176  \\\\
2a + 8 + 5a                     &= 176  \\\\
7a + 8                          &= 176  \\\\
7a                              &= 176 - 8  \\\\
7a                              &= 168  \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {168}{7}  \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {24 \times 7}{7}  \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {24 \times \cancel{7}}{\cancel{7}}  \\\\
a                               &= 24 \ \text{p}
\end{aligned}
$

Cost of a chocolate biscuit $= 24 $p

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$24 $p

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the cost of a chocolate biscuit (in pence) $= a$
<emptyline>
Cost of a bag of crisps (in pence) $= a + 4$
<emptyline>
rtq_abbr_md_unit_note
<emptyline>
Lets convert all the costs into pence (p).
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned} 
2 \times (a + 4) + 5 \times a   &= \pounds 1.76  \\\\
2 \times (a + 4) + 5 \times a   &= 1.76 \times 100 \ \text{p}  \\\\
2 \times (a + 4) + 5 \times a   &= 176  \\\\
2a + 8 + 5a                     &= 176  \\\\
7a + 8                          &= 176  \\\\
7a                              &= 176 - 8  \\\\
7a                              &= 168  \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {168}{7}  \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {24 \times 7}{7}  \\\\
a                               &= \dfrac {24 \times \cancel{7}}{\cancel{7}}  \\\\
a                               &= 24 \ \text{p}
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Cost of a chocolate biscuit $= 24 $p
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$24 $p
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<ul class='subquestion lower-alpha question_type_subquestion list_type_lower-alpha'>
<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

</div>
<div class='workings'>

&= 2 + 3 + 4 + 1 \\\\
&= 10
\end{aligned}
$

$

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

How many eggs are laid on day $8?$

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Deborah lays eggs only on alternative days. So he will not lay egg on day $8$.

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
&= 2 + 3 + 4 \\\\
&= 9
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>

<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
&= 2 + 3 + 4 \\\\
&= 9
\end{aligned}
$

</code></pre>
</div>

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

One chicken is ill on a particular day, so Farmer Burrows is only able to collect $8$ eggs.  Which chicken is ill?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Total eggs laid}                       &= 8 \\\\
\text{Total eggs that is suppose to be laid} &= 2 + 3 + 4 + 1 \\\\
                                             &= 10 \\\\
\text{Difference}                            &= 10 - 8 \\\\
                                             &= 2                                              
\end{aligned}
$

$2$ eggs are laid by Anna. 

Therefore Anna was ill that day.

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

Anna

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Total eggs laid}                       &= 8 \\\\
\text{Total eggs that is suppose to be laid} &= 2 + 3 + 4 + 1 \\\\
                                             &= 10 \\\\
\text{Difference}                            &= 10 - 8 \\\\
                                             &= 2                                              
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
$2$ eggs are laid by Anna. 
<emptyline>
Therefore Anna was ill that day.
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Anna

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

Adil answers all of the questions, leaving no blank answer spaces and gets $12$ right. What is his score?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Number of questions Adil got right} &= 12 \\\\
\text{Number of questions Adil got wrong} &= 20 - 12 \\\\
                                          &= 8 \\\\
\text{His score}                          &= 12 \times 5 - 8 \times 3 \\\\
                                          &= 60 - 24 \\\\
                                          &= 36
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$36$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Number of questions Adil got right} &= 12 \\\\
\text{Number of questions Adil got wrong} &= 20 - 12 \\\\
                                          &= 8 \\\\
\text{His score}                          &= 12 \times 5 - 8 \times 3 \\\\
                                          &= 60 - 24 \\\\
                                          &= 36
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>

</code></pre>

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

Brett answers $6$ out of the $20$ questions correctly but leaves $8$ answers spaces blank. What is his score?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Number of questions Brett got right}            &= 6 \\\\
\text{Number of questions Brett left as blank spaces} &= 8 \\\\
\text{Number of questions Brett got wrong}            &= 20 - 6 - 8 \\\\
                                                      &= 20 - 14 \\\\
                                                      &= 6 \\\\
\text{His score}                                      &= 6 \times 5 - 6 \times 3 \\\\
                                                      &= 30 - 18 \\\\
                                                      &= 12
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>

                                                      &= 6 \\\\

                                                      &= 30 - 18 \\\\
                                                      &= 12
\end{aligned}
$

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

Caroline answers all the questions and scores $28$ marks. How many questions did she get right?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number of questions Caroline got right $= a$

Nnumber of questions Caroline got wrong $= 20 - a$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
a \times 5 - (20 - a) \times 3 &= 28 \\\\
5a - 60 + 3a                   &= 28 \\\\
8a                             &= 60 + 28 \\\\
8a                             &= 88 \\\\
a                              &= \dfrac {88} {8} \\\\
a                              &= \dfrac {8 \times 11} {8} \\\\
a                              &= \dfrac {\cancel 8 \times 11} {\cancel 8} \\\\
a                              &= 11
\end{aligned}
$

Number of questions Caroline got right $= 11$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$11$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number of questions Caroline got right $= a$

<emptyline>

a                              &= \dfrac {88} {8} \\\\
a                              &= \dfrac {8 \times 11} {8} \\\\
a                              &= \dfrac {\cancel 8 \times 11} {\cancel 8} \\\\
a                              &= 11
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Number of questions Caroline got right $= 11$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>

</div>

</code></pre>
</div>

</div>
</li>
<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

David unfortunately scored zero. Give **TWO** ways in which this could have happened, **other than** him leaving **all** the answer spaces blank.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>


Let the number of questions David got right $= a$

Let the number of questions David got wrong $= b$

Number of questions David left blank $= 20 - a - b$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
a \times 5 - (20 - a) \times 3 &= 0 \\\\
5a - 60 + 3b                   &= 0 \\\\
5a + 3b                        &= 60
\end{aligned}
$

We can easily infer the values by using trial and error. Let's find out $2$ numbers which can satisfy the equations above.

| Value of a   |   Value of b      |     5a + 3b    |   
|:------------:|:-----------------:|:--------------:|
|     $1$      |         $2$       |       $11$     | 
|     $2$      |         $5$       |       $25$     |         
|     $3$      |         $15$      |       $60$     |         
|     $6$      |         $10$      |       $60$     |        

Hence, the $2$ cases in which score $0$ is possible are -

- David gets $3$ right $15$ wrong and $2$ left blank spaces

- David gets $6$ right $10$ wrong and $4$ left blank spaces

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$3, 15, 2$

</div>
<div class='answer'>

$6, 10, 4$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>
Let the number of questions David got right $= a$
<emptyline>

<emptyline>
Number of questions David left blank $= 20 - a - b$
<emptyline>

<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}

5a - 60 + 3b                   &= 0 \\\\

\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>

|     $1$      |         $2$       |       $11$     |

|     $3$      |         $15$      |       $60$     |         
|     $6$      |         $10$      |       $60$     |        
<emptyline>
Hence, the $2$ cases in which score $0$ is possible are -
<emptyline>
- David gets $3$ right $15$ wrong and $2$ left blank spaces
<emptyline>
- David gets $6$ right $10$ wrong and $4$ left blank spaces
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

Eve got twice as many right as she got wrong and scored $35$. How many answer spaces did she leave blank?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number of questions Eve got wrong $= a$

Number of questions Eve got right $= 2a$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
2a \times 5 - a \times 3 &= 35 \\\\
10a - 3a                 &= 35 \\\\
7a                       &= 35 \\\\
a                        &= \dfrac {35} {7} \\\\
a                        &= \dfrac {5 \times 7} {7} \\\\
a                        &= \dfrac {5 \times \cancel 7} {\cancel 7} \\\\
a                        &= 5
\end{aligned}
$

$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Number of questions Eve got wrong}     &= 5 \\\\
\text {Number of questions Eve got right}     &= 5 \times 2 \\\\
                                              &= 10 \\\\
\text {Number of questions Eve left as blank} &= 20 - 5 - 10 \\\\ 
                                              &= 5                                        
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$5$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number of questions Eve got wrong $= a$
<emptyline>
Number of questions Eve got right $= 2a$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
2a \times 5 - a \times 3 &= 35 \\\\
10a - 3a                 &= 35 \\\\
7a                       &= 35 \\\\
a                        &= \dfrac {35} {7} \\\\
a                        &= \dfrac {5 \times 7} {7} \\\\
a                        &= \dfrac {5 \times \cancel 7} {\cancel 7} \\\\
a                        &= 5
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Number of questions Eve got wrong}     &= 5 \\\\
\text {Number of questions Eve got right}     &= 5 \times 2 \\\\
                                              &= 10 \\\\
\text {Number of questions Eve left as blank} &= 20 - 5 - 10 \\\\ 
                                              &= 5                                        
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>

</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$5$
</code></pre>
</div>

<div class='question question'>

A shop reduces its prices by $20 \%$ in a sale. David bought a coat for $\pounds 40$ in the sale. How much would the coat have cost him **before** the sale?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the original cost of the coat (in $\pounds$) $= c$

If the price is reduced by $20 \%$, then the sale price would be $100 - 20 = 80 \%$ of the original price.

Let's use this to calculate the original price.

$
\begin{aligned}
80 \% \text{ of } c               &= 40 \\\\
\dfrac {80 } { 100 } \times c     &= 40 \\\\
c                                 &= \dfrac {40 \times 100} { 80 } \\\\
c                                 &= \dfrac {40 \times 2 \times 50} { 40 \times 2 } \\\\
c                                 &= \dfrac {\cancel{40} \times \cancel{2} \times 50} { \cancel{40} \times \cancel{2} } \\\\
c                                 &= \pounds 50 
\end{aligned}
$

Original price $= \pounds 50$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$\pounds 50$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the original cost of the coat (in $\pounds$) $= c$
<emptyline>
If the price is reduced by $20 \%$, then the sale price would be $100 - 20 = 80 \%$ of the original price.
<emptyline>
Let's use this to calculate the original price.
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
80 \% \text{ of } c               &= 40 \\\\
\dfrac {80 } { 100 } \times c     &= 40 \\\\
c                                 &= \dfrac {40 \times 100} { 80 } \\\\
c                                 &= \dfrac {40 \times 2 \times 50} { 40 \times 2 } \\\\
c                                 &= \dfrac {\cancel{40} \times \cancel{2} \times 50} { \cancel{40} \times \cancel{2} } \\\\
c                                 &= \pounds 50 
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Original price $= \pounds 50$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$\pounds 50$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<div class='question_envelope rag_g1 rag_prns question'>
<div class='uuid'>
<p>8BA1366E-EFBD-498C-A4B3-056CCEEA7FD8</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2016--arithmetic-a:1:12</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_g1</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_prns</p>
</div>
<div class='topics'>
<ul>
<li>
talgebra
</li>
<li>
tmoney
</li>
<li>
tunitprompt
</li>

<div class='question question'>

Rachel buys three identical bags of sweets and five cans of drink, the total cost of these items came to $\pounds 4.90$. 
Rosie buys just one bag of sweets and three cans of drink and pays $\pounds 2.70$.

What is the cost of a bag of sweets?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the cost of a bag of sweets $= s$

Let the cost of a can of drink $= d$

ABBR: It is really important to use the right units in calculations and convert where required
. Let's convert both the costs into pence.

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned} 
3s + 5d &= 490 \\\\
s + 3d &= 270
\end{aligned}
$

We have got $2$ variables and we have $2$ equations to solve them. Let's eliminate to reduce it to just one variable. 
But which one should we eliminate? If we look at the question again, we only need the 
cost for a bag of sweets, so let's eliminate $d$ so we can directly calculate the value for $s$.

The LCM of $5$ and $3$ is $15$. Multiply the first equation by $3$ and the second equation by $5$ to make the coeifficent of $d$ the same.

$
\begin{aligned} 
3 \times 3s + 3 \times 5d   &= 3 \times 490 \\\\
5 \times  s + 5 \times 3d   &= 5 \times 270 \\\\
9s + 15d                    &= 1470 \\\\
5s + 15d                    &= 1350
\end{aligned}
$

Subtract the second equation from the first to reduce it to one variable.

$
\begin{aligned} 
9s + 15d -(5s + 15d)               &= 1470 - 1350 \\\\
9s + 15d -5s - 15d                 &= 120 \\\\
4s                                 &= 120 \\\\
s                                  &= \dfrac{120}{4} \\\\
s                                  &= 30\ \text{p}
\end{aligned}
$

Cost of a bag of sweets $= 30\ \text{p}$

</div>
<div class='working'>

What if we eliminate $s$ instead?

This approach is less efficient as we will have to calculate one extra value to get the answer.

Let's eliminate to reduce it to just one variable. 

Multiply the second equation by $3$.

$
\begin{aligned} 
3 \times s + 3 \times 3d   &= 3 \times 270 \\\\
3s + 9d                     &= 810
\end{aligned}
$

Subtract the first equation from the second to reduce it to one variable.

$
\begin{aligned} 
3s + 5d                     &= 490 \\\\
3s + 9d                     &= 810 \\\\
3s + 9d - (3s + 5d)         &= 810 - 490 \\\\
3s + 9d -3s - 5d            &= 320 \\\\
4d                          &= 320 \\\\
d                           &= \dfrac {320} {4} \\\\
d                           &= 80\ \text{p}
\end{aligned}
$

Cost of a can of drink $= 80\ \text{p}$ 

Let's substitute the value of $d$ in one of the initial equations to find the cost of a bag of sweets.

$
\begin{aligned} 
3s + 5d             &= 490 \\\\
3s + 5 \times 80    &= 490 \\\\
3s + 400            &= 490 \\\\
3s                  &= 490 - 400 \\\\
3s                  &= 90 \\\\
s                   &= \dfrac {90} {3}\\\\
s                   &= 30\ \text{p}
\end{aligned}
$

Cost of a bag of sweets $= 30\ \text{p}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$30\ \text{p}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the cost of a bag of sweets $= s$
<emptyline>
Let the cost of a can of drink $= d$
<emptyline>
rtq_abbr_md_unit_no_dot_note. Let's convert both the costs into pence.
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}

s + 3d &= 270
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>

But which one should we eliminate? If we look at the question again, we only need the

<emptyline>

<emptyline>

\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Subtract the second equation from the first to reduce it to one variable.
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}

9s + 15d -5s - 15d                 &= 120 \\\\
4s                                 &= 120 \\\\
s                                  &= \dfrac{120}{4} \\\\
s                                  &= 30\ \text{p}
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Cost of a bag of sweets $= 30\ \text{p}$
</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>What if we eliminate $s$ instead?
<emptyline>

<emptyline>

\end{aligned}
$

<li class='questionContainer'>
<div class='question_envelope rag_g1 rag_prns question'>
<div class='uuid'>
<p>E2B23658-50CC-43B0-813D-B11A7DDE570B</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2016--arithmetic-a:1:13</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>Waffles Woof</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_g1</p>
</div>
<div class='rag'>
<p>rag_wf_prns</p>

<div class='topics'>
<ul>
<li>
talgebra
</li>
<li>
tstatistics
</li>
</ul>
</div>
<div class='question question'>

Tim has three apples. The mean (average) weight of the three apples is $90 \ \text {grams}$. When Tim eats the largest apple, the mean weight of the remaining two apples is just $70 \ \text {grams}$.

What was the weight of the largest apple?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the weight for the largest apple (in $\text{grams}$) $= a$

$\text{Average weight} = \dfrac {\text{Total Weight}} {\text{Total Apples}}$

$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Mean weight of } 2 \text{ apples}     &= 70 \\\\
\text{Total weight of } 2 \text{ apples}    &= 70 \times 2 \\\\
                                            &= 140  \ \text {grams}
\end{aligned}
$

Using the average weight for $3$ apples, we get

$
\begin{aligned}
\dfrac {a + 140} {3}    &= 90  \\\\
a + 140                 &= 90  \times 3 \\\\
a + 140                 &= 270 \\\\
a                       &= 270 - 140\\\\
a                       &= 130 \ \text {grams} 
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$130 \ \text {grams}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the weight for the largest apple (in $\text{grams}$) $= a$
<emptyline>
$\text{Average weight} = \dfrac {\text{Total Weight}} {\text{Total Apples}}$
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Mean weight of } 2 \text{ apples}     &= 70 \\\\
\text{Total weight of } 2 \text{ apples}    &= 70 \times 2 \\\\
                                            &= 140  \ \text {grams}
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Using the average weight for $3$ apples, we get
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
\dfrac {a + 140} {3}    &= 90  \\\\
a + 140                 &= 90  \times 3 \\\\
a + 140                 &= 270 \\\\
a                       &= 270 - 140\\\\
a                       &= 130 \ \text {grams} 
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$130 \ \text {grams}$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<div class='question question'>

In the sequence of numbers which starts $2, 3, 5, 9, \ldots$  each number is one less than two times the previous number in the list. 

What is the number in the list immediately **before** $257?$

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number immediately before $= a$

As per the question,


$
\begin{aligned} 
2a - 1      &= 257 \\\\
2a          &= 257 + 1 \\\\
2a          &= 258 \\\\
a           &= \dfrac {129 * 2}{2} \\\\
a           &= \dfrac {129 * \cancel 2}{\cancel 2} \\\\
a           &= 129
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$129$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number immediately before $= a$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>

$
\begin{aligned} 
2a - 1      &= 257 \\\\
2a          &= 257 + 1 \\\\
2a          &= 258 \\\\
a           &= \dfrac {129 * 2}{2} \\\\
a           &= \dfrac {129 * \cancel 2}{\cancel 2} \\\\
a           &= 129
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>

</div>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$129$
</code></pre>

<div class='uuid'>
<p>0B47659C-81C5-46B4-8F2D-C1372D5BCBAD</p>
</div>
<div class='papername'>
<p>the-manchester-grammar-school--11-plus--maths--2016--arithmetic-a:1:15</p>

<div class='rag'>

talgebra
